TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

The Malian military and its Russian allies retook Kidal last week after a tactical withdrawal by Tuareg separatists, who had captured the strategic city in April. According to some analysts, Algeria may have played a role in convincing the rebels to withdraw. The developments come amid a resumption of bilateral ties with Algiers, which reopened its airspace to Bamako in July, ending a 15-month rift following a dispute over Algeria shooting down a Malian drone along their shared border. Algeria, the second-strongest military power in Africa after Egypt, served for decades as a peace broker between Tuareg rebels and Mali’s government. In recent months, it has aggressively sought to reclaim this role—alongside its traditional status as a security mediator in the Sahel—amid escalating security threats across the region. Although Algeria’s foreign policy maintains a strategy of noninterference in other states’ internal affairs, it has historically viewed the Sahel and West Africa as its backyard. “They’ve always sort of looked at the Sahel as part of the general geopolitical area in which they exert their influence,” said Intissar Fakir, a senior fellow and the founding director of the North Africa and Sahel program at the Middle East Institute. With Mali, Fakir added, “there’s a lot of shared interests in terms of the Tuareg issue, in terms of counterterrorism.” In particular, Algeria fears insurgency spilling over into its southern regions among its own Tuareg populations, according to some analysts. But in recent years, as Algeria’s role in the Sahel has waned, it has become increasingly worried about foreign actors, including Russia and the United Arab Emirates , pushing for greater influence in the region. “Algeria began to find itself quite isolated in its own neighborhood,” Fakir said. Algeria has long disapproved of Mali’s use of Russian mercenaries, despite Algiers’s good relations with Moscow. It is not clear whether Hegseth, a man who seems to take crudeness as a virtue, researched the history of state executions before going public with this plan. If he had, he would have quickly learned two things that would give a sensible and morally informed defense secretary pause: The U.S. military hasn’t performed a public execution in 90 years, and the countries that continue to enact rituals like these, such as Iran and Afghanistan under the Taliban, head the list of nations that the United States has long routinely deplored as barbaric. Credit Hegseth for understanding his boss, though. The government insists that only those who don’t belong on the rolls were removed. On Oct. 10, security forces in New Delhi detained as many as 7,000 people, and authorities closed metro stations and shut off mobile internet services. The protests, coming on the heels of mass protests against India’s education policies this summer, threaten to become the biggest sustained street mobilization against Prime Minister Narendra Modi in his 12 years in power. The protests—taking place in large cities across the country—are unlikely to die down anytime soon, but it’s worth putting the movement in perspective. Though tens of thousands of people have mobilized so far, in a country of India’s size, this doesn’t equate to a truly national mass movement. More Indians protested against sexual assault in 2012 and 2013 and corruption in 2011—all during the rule of Modi’s predecessor, former Prime Minister Manmohan Singh of the Indian National Congress party. There is also a big difference between the demonstrations in India and the mass movements that ousted governments in Bangladesh, Nepal, and Sri Lanka in recent years: Modi’s government remains popular. Though many Indians—especially young ones—are increasingly angry about misgovernance and economic stress, a critical mass of the population still backs Modi. However, Modi can’t simply shrug off the ongoing protests and assume that things will settle down.   https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/10/14/india-protests-voter-roll-cockroach-modi-bjp/

* Environmentalists Are Wrong to Oppose the Senate’s Permitting Deal
  Foreign Policy | 14 Oct 20:51 UTC
  America needs to build more to achieve its energy goals of lower costs, greater security, and more clean energy.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. From soaring diesel prices to Europe’s devastating summer heat waves, threats to energy security and the climate are intensifying. The introduction of U.S. permitting reform legislation on Sept. 30 brought a rare opportunity to address both. Leaders of the Senate’s two main energy and environment committees reached a bipartisan agreement on the most significant infrastructure overhaul in decades. The compromise is a major achievement, among the most important policy measures in decades to advance clean energy, meet rising energy needs, and rein in soaring energy prices. Rejecting it purely because it may ease construction of fossil fuel projects, as many in the environmental movement are advocating , would be an error. Energy policy requires balancing the often-competing needs of security, affordability, and sustainability. Today, achieving each of these ambitions depends on the United States’ ability to build. The reality is that while the bill may lead to some additional fossil fuel projects and related emissions in the United States, those will be offset by lower production elsewhere and outweighed by longer-term gains from the expansion of clean energy. And in that sense, the bill is very likely to be a net positive for the environment. More than 30,000 paramilitary troopers have now been deployed to the capital. Some of these forces were seen violently dragging demonstrators to police vans, including members of Parliament . Evidence of excessive force circulated widely online. Despite the crackdown, thousands of people continued to turn up in New Delhi to protest with greater vigor. These demonstrations, galvanized in part by a youth-led movement, have escalated into a broader referendum on Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi. Confronted with political fatigue, a falling rupee, and foreign-policy disappointments, Modi’s government has abandoned political engagement in favor of this theater of state coercion . With formal democratic institutions weakened, tens of thousands of Indians are in the streets—seemingly recognizing that defending the vote is an existential fight for their democracy. * The Painful Paradox of European Rearmament
  Foreign Policy | 14 Oct 14:58 UTC
  Militarizing now may require even further militarization down the line.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. European countries have had a rude awakening over the last five years or so. The Russian invasion of Ukraine and its escalating acts of hybrid warfare have exposed the limits of civilian power and underscored the importance of hard military capabilities. The process began in 2022, when Sweden and Finland announced their intention to join NATO and then-German Chancellor Olaf Scholz proclaimed a dramatic reversal ( Zeitenwende ) of Germany’s security policy. Since then, most European states have begun to formulate ambitious rearmament plans. The Trump administration’s open hostility toward the European Union, a gradual reduction in the U.S. military presence on the continent, and President Donald Trump’s tendency to favor Russia over Ukraine have made the need for greater European capacity even clearer. Instead of counting on Uncle Sam to be the first responder when trouble occurs, European states will have to rely on their own resources and strategies to keep themselves safe. Not surprisingly, this sense of alarm is most evident in the states that lie closest to Russia (e.g., the Baltic states, Poland, etc.) and less visible in those that are farther away, such as Portugal or Spain. Far-right parties like the Alternative for Germany remain sympathetic to Moscow, but France’s National Rally has recently become more critical of Russia’s actions toward Europe, if only in an attempt to accommodate French opinion. The process began in 2022, when Sweden and Finland announced their intention to join NATO and then-German Chancellor Olaf Scholz proclaimed a dramatic reversal ( Zeitenwende ) of Germany’s security policy. Since then, most European states have begun to formulate ambitious rearmament plans. The Trump administration’s open hostility toward the European Union, a gradual reduction in the U.S. military presence on the continent, and President Donald Trump’s tendency to favor Russia over Ukraine have made the need for greater European capacity even clearer. Their answer to that question could determine the election outcome. Haredim largely resist compulsory military service, and their contribution to the formal economy remains limited. Although many Haredi women work, a large proportion of men devote themselves to Torah study. The community has fiercely guarded its way of life, leveraging its political influence in coalition governments and presenting itself as the guardian of Jewish spirituality. Since Oct. 7, 2023, however, Israel has fought prolonged wars in Gaza and Lebanon as well as a confrontation with Iran, increasing the demand for military personnel. Non-Haredi Israelis have become increasingly vocal in opposing what they see as an unfair distribution of the burden. Must they risk their lives while Haredi men remain at home? Many see Haredi life as a privilege in times of war—and one that the country can no longer afford. Divisions within Israeli society have deepened, particularly as the Israel Defense Forces (IDF) has warned of a shortage of as many as 12,000 soldiers and called on the ultra-Orthodox to help fill the gap. Around 80,000 Haredim aged 18-24 are reportedly believed to be eligible for military service but have not enlisted. * After Iran, Washington Needs a New Basing Strategy
  Foreign Policy | 14 Oct 09:00 UTC

  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. For a generation, the United States has relied on large military bases across the Persian Gulf to project power, confident that they were beyond the reach of groups such as the Islamic State and that Iran, despite its growing missile arsenal, would not risk a sustained attack on them. The Iran war has shattered that assumption, with Iranian missiles and drones repeatedly striking those bases and forcing the U.S. military to empty facilities it has operated for decades. Now the Pentagon is weighing whether to rebuild and harden those bases or shift its force presence westward , including to Jordan and Israel, farther from Iran’s reach. It should do neither. * Kraken Owner Payward to Buy Lithuania's Saldo Bank in EU Push
  Decrypt | 14 Oct 10:21 UTC
  Co-CEO Arjun Sethi says customers want cash, crypto, tokenized stocks and credit in one place, starting with lending and cards.
  FULL TEXT: Payward, the parent company of crypto exchange Kraken, is buying Lithuania-based Saldo Bank as it expands into lending and card issuing, the Financial Times reported on Wednesday. Payward is the first digital asset company to buy a bank in Europe, according to the FT . Saldo received its EU banking license in 2021 and offers consumer and business loans as well as term deposits. The price was not disclosed. "One of the biggest things that our customers have been asking us is 'I would like to get products and services and offerings similar to what I get with another bank,'" Payward co-CEO Arjun Sethi told the FT . Sethi said the plan is to bring cash, crypto, tokenized stocks and credit together in a single system, so customers can borrow and lend against their assets without selling them. The Wyoming-based company, founded in 2011, applied in May for a national trust company charter from the U.S. Office of the Comptroller of the Currency, the federal bank regulator. Coinbase and Ripple are among the crypto firms that have received conditional approval for such charters, which let them hold customers' assets and provide transaction services but not take deposits. This month, the Independent Community Bankers of America sued the OCC, arguing that granting such charters to crypto firms exceeds the regulator's authority. "Any non-fiduciary firm seeking the benefits of a federal bank charter should meet the same standards as community banks," ICBA President and CEO Rebecca Romero Rainey said in a statement. An OCC spokesperson declined to comment. The bank deal follows Payward's $550 million acquisition of derivatives exchange Bitnomial in April and its $600 million purchase of Asian stablecoin payments firm Reap Technologies in May. Kraken filed confidentially for an IPO last November, but has since pushed the listing back to the second quarter of 2027 at the earliest, CoinDesk reported in September, citing people familiar with the matter. The Saldo acquisition is subject to regulatory approvals and is expected to close in 2027. Start every day with the top news stories right now, plus original features, a podcast, videos and more.
  https://decrypt.co/380912/kraken-owner-payward-to-buy-lithuanias-saldo-bank-in-eu-push

Baskin provides a fascinating glimpse into a world where adolescents can express themselves and explore their identities. Bafflement is the baseline condition of raising kids right now. The adolescent quest for self-discovery is timeless—but today’s parents experimented in ways that were analog: new hairstyles, inscrutable music, beer snuck in the woods. Their own parents might not have liked it, but they could at least relate. Now the landscape of adolescent discovery is literally foreign, thanks to social media, which has turbocharged once-obscure subcultures like the furry fandom—and, at its rarer edge, therianthropy—and redefined what “fringe” even means. Even if a kid is the only therian at school, she can find 400 more people just like her online. That’s one of the biggest challenges for parents in the algorithm age: The world your kid actually lives in is one you can’t enter. It touches the fear in every parent, therian kid or not: What happens when our kids inhabit a world we don’t understand, with idols we don’t recognize? “They spend their lives seeking seconds of darkness.” “The once underground and countercultural ‘rave’ has fully spread to the mainstream in the form of themed pop-ups and sponsored parties. As of September 2026, the Egyptian pound had lost about 70% of its value against the dollar since early 2022, according to the Central Bank of Egypt. For paper suppliers like Abdelrahman, who sells finished paper products primarily from China and Indonesia, regional shipping disruptions have caused the cost of imported goods to rise unabated; Houthi attacks in the Bab al-Mandab Strait forced many shipping companies to suspend transit through the Suez Canal in late 2023, and the disruption of oil shipments through the Strait of Hormuz during the U.S.-Israel war on Iran has further raised importing costs. While the Bab al-Mandab Strait has remained open amid Houthi advances in September 2026, shipping traffic has declined. India had nearly a billion voters during the 2024 national elections. Now, over 130 million Indians, more than the population of countries such as Japan, Egypt and the Philippines, have been left off the draft voter rolls in an exercise called the Special Intensive Revision, or SIR. While election officers went to the homes of the powerful to verify their papers and bring them back onto the voter roll, ordinary Indians have been running from pillar to post to have their names counted. Sounam and her siblings don’t know what to do next. “Our brother was forced to prove his Indianness until his last breath. Lanphear printed out a copy of an old deed linking the Edgewood land to the Phelps and Gorham Company, a group of New England speculators. For a bargain price, Phelps and Gorham had acquired the title to 2.6 million acres of forested country along the Genesee River, in what is now western New York. The company then hired Maxwell—a Revolutionary War veteran, father of seven, and the first recorded white person to pass within shouting distance of the place where I now live—as surveyor. As Maxwell crossed the forest, he imagined the nation he believed would one day replace it. “The land in this country is exceedingly good, but it wants good inhabitants,” Maxwell wrote to his wife, Bridget, imagining “many worshipping assemblies of Christians where now the wild beasts howl.” In place of the forest, he envisioned schools, churches, cash crops, and county clerks. With scarcely a thought, I switch from one app to another, scrolling through images and opinions and adverts for no discernible reason beyond habit. Later, I head for my office and open my laptop to a slew of half-completed documents and an inbox brimming with emails. My stress levels begin to nudge upwards. I have vague memories of what my professional life felt like before smartphones and video calls. I used to do my writing by hand. And I could concentrate for hours at a time, getting little upticks of arousal from lifting my head from the page, letting the hold on my pen loosen, and gazing at the life beyond my window, the light softening on my skin and eyes like snowfall on warm tarmac. Nowadays, my productive bursts come from caffeine and sugary or salty snacks, even if I try to pick the healthier ones, and from checking my emails or picking up my phone to update my inbox. By the end of the day, the sheer volume of work I expect myself to do, now that digital tools make so much possible, has kept me nailed to my seat. Another day has passed and the hope I’d had that I might have time for a run or a hike outside has been dashed again. I wish I could say that this is atypical. Yet, during the COVID-19 pandemic, I went from hours spent in nature and on physical activities to a passive, phone-addicted individual, meeting people first and sometimes only online. I am not alone. We have fallen into habits that are steadily deskilling the human body, eroding capacities honed by evolution to support our wellbeing as we move through the world. Some of these capacities are weakening through disuse: we have come to rely on our phones, for example, and now, increasingly, on AI, to rapidly do work that would once have required something more physical – like looking around, or asking someone, or going to find and consult a book. Reliance on GPS may weaken both our spatial attention and the fluency of our wayfinding. In surrendering to a quick technological fix, we are, moment by moment, doing less sensing, navigating, remembering, walking, choosing, and attending. In the evenings, groups of students headed downtown in search of entertainment or a decent meal, tired of the food served at the college. During the first semester of their final year at college, hundreds of aspiring teachers were assigned to local schools, where they spent an entire semester teaching, putting into practice what they had learned and preparing for their future careers. The large number of trainee teachers helped fill staffing shortages at local schools, while also allowing regular teachers to take a break from their teaching duties. Visiting recently, I was struck by the starkly contrasting scenes at the college. The classrooms were empty, the corridors silent, and the dormitories were gathering dust, with their windows and doors either deteriorating or missing altogether. I was told that similar scenes are unfolding at colleges of education and other teacher training institutions across Yemen, including in Aden, Taiz, Shabwa, al-Mahra and Hadramawt. Many have been forced to phase out departments because enrollment has plummeted, from hundreds of students more than a decade ago to zero in some departments today. * Is everything conscious?
  Aeon | 12 Oct 10:00 UTC
  If the question makes no sense to you, try spending some time with the principle of sufficient reason - by Sam Kimpton-Nye Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: When an explanation makes a fact intelligible, the mystery goes out of it. We understand it so well that we can hardly conceive of things being otherwise. Consider the simple mathematical fact that one plus one equals two. Once we understand elementary arithmetic, we no longer feel the urge to ask why one plus one equals two. That one plus one equals two strikes us as not merely true but unavoidable; we cannot even coherently imagine its falsity. Much of philosophy aims to bring other fundamental features of reality into a similar light, to make them appear not arbitrary but inevitable and, thus, intelligible. This aspiration that philosophy render the world intelligible is best expressed by the German polymath Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz and the Dutch philosopher Baruch Spinoza . According to Leibniz: ‘we can find no true or existent fact … without there being a sufficient reason why it is thus and not otherwise.’ And, as Spinoza wrote: ‘For each thing there must be assigned a cause or reason, both for its existence and for its nonexistence.’ What’s being expressed here, in the words of the US philosopher Michael Della Rocca, is the desire ‘to make the world and our place in it intelligible’. To grasp the kinds of reasons or explanations sought by Leibniz and Spinoza ‘is the perennial hope and promise of philosophy’. The demand for intelligibility can lead to some extraordinary places. For Leibniz, the demand led him to propose that reality is ultimately made up of a multiplicity of monads: indivisible, infinitesimally small units of substance, each of which is a causally isolated, self-contained universe. Spinoza’s resolute commitment to universal intelligibility led him to conclude that reality is, instead, a single infinite substance that he called God or nature. As we will see, the demand for intelligibility can also lead into the strange and fascinating world of panpsychism. Panpsychism is an increasingly popular view about consciousness. You are conscious, you feel pleasures, pains and all manner of other experiences. At every waking moment, and even when you dream, there is something that it is like to be you. The same goes for other humans and animals, and this accounts for moral status: thumping a fist on a desk in frustration is morally permissible in a way that hitting a conscious person or animal is not. Desks, chairs, rocks and so on are not conscious – and are therefore without moral status – whereas humans, bonobos, dogs and so on are conscio
  https://aeon.co/essays/does-the-principle-of-sufficient-reason-make-panpsychism-true

* A Rupture in the History of Consciousness
  New Lines | 09 Oct 10:33 UTC
  Over the centuries, layers of interpretation have mediated our experience and separated the self from the world, but glimpses of an older mode of consciousness can still be found. Even modern literature that is transfixed by the self and immersed in its workings can fleetingly reveal the older intimacy. The post A Rupture in the History o...
  FULL TEXT: In sixth-century Arabia, a man wakes up at night and begins to saddle his she-camel, something he must have done countless times, and she’s tired of it. The man is al-Muthaqqib al-Abdi, a pre-Islamic Arab poet whose three surviving poems have been acclaimed by classical and modern critics alike. In this poem, we glimpse a rare form of consciousness, in which the borders between man and camel almost disappear. In the poet’s mind, they coexist within a shared matrix of existence that doesn’t split the world into subject and object, into the human being standing here and nature out there. Al-Abdi says in his poem (my translation is informed in part by the rendering of the late-19th- and early-20th-century scholar C. J. Lyall): When I rise at night to saddle her, she sighs the sigh of a sorrowing man. As I lay out the girth for her, she says: “Is this forever to be his way, and mine? Must all of life be stopping and setting out again? Will he never spare me or keep me from harm?” To me, the ingenuity of these lines lies in their directness, honesty and lack of imposed poetic devices, despite the highly structured verse. The poet, the camel and the journey itself share a seamless order of being while also remaining distinct from one another. Their knowledge of each other is so intimate that he reads her groan as a sigh that reveals her suffering to him. Yet move he must, as the harsh realities of the desert demand. The coming of Islam ruptured that shared matrix and the worldview that came with it, calling on people to take stock of the world and see in it signs pointing to the one God. But this new way of seeing never fully destroyed the earlier mode of perception that had been present during the Jahiliyya — the name given to the pre-Islamic period of Arabian history, literally meaning the “age of ignorance.” Some Bedouin herders still form close attachments to camels they know by name, a present-day trace of that older intimacy, similar to the way that many in the Arab world and beyond bond with their horses; I saw some of these interactions firsthand when I lived in the UAE. Such intimacy could persist even as abstract concepts and dissecting principles gained authority, pulling humans away from their surroundings and setting them at a distance. Greek philosophers also distanced us from that older state of consciousness, attempting to unearth principles that lie beneath appearances.   Aeon | 09 Oct 10:00 UTC
  She was among the first to argue that prosperity, equality and the protection of nature are inseparable planetary goals - by Or Rosenboim Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Barbara Ward at the UN Conference on the Human Environment in Stockholm, Sweden, in 1972. Courtesy UN Photo In 1957, the economist and journalist Barbara Ward gave the first of the Aggrey-Fraser-Guggisberg Memorial Lectures at the University College of Ghana. Shortly after Kwame Nkrumah led the British colony of the Gold Coast towards self-government, he invited Ward to address the independent country’s first cohort of students. Ward was well acquainted with the local political landscape because she had spent the previous four years living in the Gold Coast, witnessing its political transformation. As the world’s eyes were set on Ghana, the first sub-Saharan colony to achieve independence from European rule, Ward was determined to provide her audience with the knowledge needed to govern their newly founded country. Her lectures, titled Five Ideas that Change the World , developed a world-historical narrative of the supposed human progress from nationalism to colonialism, industrialism, communism, and finally to internationalism, the highest point of political order. Ghana’s peaceful democratic transition showed that African colonies were ready to govern themselves, weakening European arguments about the need for prolonged imperial guidance. The country soon became a model for new African statehood based on postcolonial sovereignty, inspiring other colonies on the continent to join the decolonisation process, which culminated in the ‘Year of Africa’ in 1960. The charismatic Nkrumah, who ruled Ghana from 1957 to 1966, became an international player in Cold War diplomacy by promoting pan-Africanist visions of post-imperial unity. He wanted to show the world that Africans were no longer imperial subjects but important and influential members of the global political community. While Nkrumah was a vociferous opponent of colonialism, Ward uncritically appreciated the civilisational and technical legacies of empire. Yet in her lectures, she supported Nkrumah’s international political aspirations by suggesting that economic development, international cooperation, and even world peace were interrelated problems that warranted a planetary solution. B arbara Ward was a remarkable thinker, a visionary who imagined a ‘planetary’ political, economic and environmental justice based on both material and spiritual principles. She was an unusual figure in the Cold War political sphere, a charismatic public intellectual who advised world leaders and the United Nations at a ti
  https://aeon.co/essays/why-we-should-heed-barbara-wards-vision-of-planetary-justice

  FULL TEXT: Like many Tigrayans, I live in exile from my homeland, Ethiopia’s northernmost region, bordering Eritrea. We are a people scattered by war. Since mid-August, we have been hearing about bombs falling on Tigray, attacks carried out on a near-daily basis by Ethiopian fighter jets and drones. Those of us who are lucky enough to live outside can only hope that our families and friends are safe. But it is hard to know for sure, since telecommunications have been shut down. I try to comfort myself by listening to music from the past. I keep returning to Mulat Hayelom’s “Selam Tigray” (“Peace to Tigray”), an old song that for me remains timeless and transcendental, even through the tinny speakers of my phone. “If your land is at peace, my homeland, you are desirable,” Mulat sings. “But in July and August bombs fell on your land instead of rain. Those who decided on war for you, my country, may they lose their eyes.” Which leaders, I ask myself, deserve to be eyeless? Because someone keeps deciding on war for Tigray, time after time. My grandfather used to tell me stories of the 1930s, when Ethiopia was invaded by Mussolini’s fascist army, and Tigray was bombed with mustard gas. My father lived through the horrors of the long war against the Derg, a brutal military dictatorship that seized power in Ethiopia in 1974. In 1991, the Derg was overthrown by the Tigray People’s Liberation Front (TPLF), a grassroots rebel movement with mass popular support. You will not find a single family in Tigray that did not lose someone during those 17 years of armed struggle. The TPLF’s victory was supposed to usher in a new era of peace and development. But in 1998 it led Ethiopia into a devastating border war against Eritrea. My uncle fought and died in that conflict, one death among tens of thousands on both sides. My turn came in 2020. That year Ethiopia’s federal government — led by an upstart prime minister named Abiy Ahmed — joined forces with Eritrea and militias from the Amhara region to wage war on Tigray. The conflict was merciless from the start. Civilians were rounded up and massacred. Women were corralled and methodically raped. In Western Tigray, nearly a million Tigrayans were expelled from their land by the Amhara militias, whose leaders openly declared their intent
  https://newlinesmag.com/first-person/the-betrayal-of-tigray/

* Life in Iran’s War-Torn South on the Strait of Hormuz
  New Lines | 08 Oct 10:32 UTC
  A writer journeys through Hormozgan province, from Bandar Abbas fish market to the graves of Minab's schoolchildren and a bombed wedding in Kuhestak, where residents say the ceasefire never arrived. The post Life in Iran’s War-Torn South on the Strait of Hormuz appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: At nearly 104 degrees Fahrenheit and 80% humidity, Bandar Abbas was already an open-air sauna at 5 a.m., as fishers unloaded their fresh catch of shrimp and fish at the city’s wholesale fish market, right at the wharf. The intense tang of rotten fish residue was unbearable. Elias Rahimi, like other wholesalers, had placed a tiny wooden desk in the middle of the covered marketplace. A calculator, a card reader, a notebook and a record of sales from recent days filled the desk, behind which he sat on a red plastic chair. Next to him stood a heavy-duty platform scale. Loads of tuna and queenfish lay in rows on the tiled floor around him. Each day, clients show up at his desk, weigh fish, and make purchases or settle sales. Rahimi uses a phosphor-green highlight marker to strike the deal. Then the clients put their notebooks on his desk to have the payment confirmation stamped in them. The ritual acts make him resemble a judge. By 6 a.m. he had already sold 2 tons of fish. “My sales have dropped by 30% since last year, but thank God the business is still running well,” Rahimi said, with the typical relaxed southern Iranian accent, elongating the words’ last vowel. In his view, the rapid inflation and loss of purchasing power of the population have contributed to the drop, in addition to the maritime siege initiated by the U.S. Navy, which means fishers “cannot sail into deep waters, resulting in smaller catches and less variety. Numerous times they’ve struck vessels when they went farther.” By 7 a.m., as the humidity rose and the heat became unbearable, Rahimi locked up his chair and desk and left his mobile office at the country’s largest loading site for seafood, which is a major economic lifeline for southern Iran, with an estimated annual yield of more than 800,000 tons. The marketplace is only a stone’s throw away from the parking space of a nearby vessel that was struck twice on July 8 and 9 this year. More than 30 boats and their fishing accessories were destroyed. While the half-ruined building is being used again, a burnt roof, cracked murals, melted fishing nets, torn ropes and flat cart tires vividly testify to the risks the fishers endure. Less than 5 nautical miles from Bandar Abbas, arguably Iran’s main gate to the Strait of Hormuz, lie burnt Iranian navy frigates and container ships such as MSC Aries, its bridge bearing explosion marks.   https://newlinesmag.com/reportage/life-in-irans-war-torn-south-on-the-strait-of-hormuz/

She offers advice on the practice of reporting and storytelling and the business of freelancing, from balancing interiority and research to building a story’s structure to pitching stories . For The Verge , she reported on an obsessive stalker who harassed Asian American professors online . Her narrative nonfiction illuminates the lives of individuals and communities, and the human connections within them. * The ghetto on the lagoon
  Aeon | 08 Oct 10:00 UTC
  Sixteenth-century Venice wanted to expel its Jews but couldn’t do without them. Its compromise was the world’s first ghetto - by Alexander Lee Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Ghetto Novo, Venice, November 2018. Photo by Antonio Violi/Alamy On 29 March 1516, the Venetian Senate voted to create the world’s first ‘ghetto’. All Jews then living in the city – as well as any others who might come later – were ordered to move immediately to a small island in the northeastern sestiere of Cannaregio, a neighbourhood known as the geto novo . It was anything but appealing. Previously used as a dumping ground for the communal copper foundry, it had been converted into a residential quarter for local workers. From the outside, it already looked ‘like a castle’ – ringed by canals and bounded by perilously tall tenements. But by the time Venice’s Jews were herded in, it seemed more like a prison. All outward-facing doors and windows were blocked up; extra walls were built to close off access to the water; and a pair of sturdy iron gates were placed at either end of the bridge leading to Cannaregio. These were closed at sunset and opened in the morning at the ringing of the marangona bell. And just to be sure that no one could sneak out when they weren’t supposed to, four guards – paid for by the Jews themselves – patrolled the canals around the Ghetto ‘day and night’. View of Venice ( c 1500) by Jacopo de’ Barbari, just prior to the Ghetto’s establishment; the location of the Ghetto is outlined. Courtesy Wikipedia It was not the first time that Jews had ever been confined to a separate quarter, of course. By the early 16th century, Jews in France and in German lands had already been living in designated neighbourhoods, physically distinct from the surrounding areas, for close on 300 years. Nor was it even the first time Venice itself had toyed with the idea of Jewish confinement. Back in the 13th century, it had already obliged Jews in its Cretan colony’s capital Candia (now Heraklion) to live in a separate area. What made the Ghetto unusual was its name. Etymologically speaking, of course, the word geto had nothing to do with Jews. Derived from the verb gettare , meaning ‘to throw’ or ‘cast’, it was just the local name for a foundry ( geto ) – and, by extension, for the dump next door. But even after the Venetian government moved the foundry elsewhere, people kept on calling the area by its old name. And when the Jews moved in, the island soon lent its name to the institution of segregation, too – a bit like how, today, we can use ‘the White House’ to refer both to 1600 Pennsylvania Avenue and to the administration that’s based there.   https://aeon.co/essays/the-making-of-the-ghetto-on-a-venetian-island-in-1516
